# Proposed OEIS comments: A207594 to A208842

One section per OEIS entry: the date the result was obtained, the paper that proves it, what the entry still records as unsettled, and the text proposed for posting on that entry.

**Nothing here has been posted.** See [../SUBMITTING.md](../SUBMITTING.md).

---

## A207594

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A207594>  
Result obtained: **31 August 2026**  
Paper: [6416](../papers/6001-6500/6416-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 25*a(n-2) +2*a(n-3) -199*a(n-4) +632*a(n-6) -30*a(n-7) -879*a(n-8) +28*a(n-9) +548*a(n-10) -139*a(n-12) -4*a(n-13) +10*a(n-14) for n>16

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 625 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 16. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

---

## A207596

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A207596>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [2949](../papers/2501-3000/2949-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 30*n^3 + 15*n^2 - 45*n + 15.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Jun 25 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 241 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

---

## A207597

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A207597>  
Result obtained: **1 September 2026**  
Paper: [2714](../papers/2501-3000/2714-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 25*n^4 + 50*n^3 - 25*n^2 - 50*n + 25.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Jun 25 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical closed form is correct. The count is a walk count in a finite graph, so it satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence; the conjectured form satisfies the same one, and the two agree at enough consecutive indices to agree from then on, for n > 5. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

Result obtained: **31 August 2026**  
Paper: [7243](../papers/7001-7500/7243-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = a(n-1) + 23*a(n-3) + 6*a(n-4) + 6*a(n-5) - 108*a(n-6) - 72*a(n-7) + 216*a(n-9) for n>11.
> Empirical g.f.: x*(12 + 132*x + 276*x^2 + 1247*x^3 + 625*x^4 - 324*x^5 - 7377*x^6 - 9090*x^7 - 846*x^8 + 17064*x^9 + 7884*x^10) / (1 - x - 23*x^3 - 6*x^4 - 6*x^5 + 108*x^6 + 72*x^7 - 216*x^9). - _Colin Barker_, Jun 26 2018

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 144 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 11. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

Proposed OEIS comment:

```

```

## A208347

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A208347>  
Result obtained: **7 September 2026**  
Paper: [8924](../papers/8501-9000/8924-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 10*a(n-1) -9*a(n-2) for n>4

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Every cell the condition names lies a bounded number of lines away -- two of them, where it reaches a knight's move -- so a window of that width decides the condition on the line in the middle, and the lines with nothing far enough beyond them are decided at the end. Counting a line at a time therefore gives a transfer matrix with 5 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 4. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

What the entry records as unsettled:

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
Not only empirical: the generating function given above is algebraic, and a recurrence with polynomial coefficients is a differential operator applied to it. Reducing that operator leaves a polynomial of degree 0, so the recurrence holds for n > 0. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A208375

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A208375>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [3194](../papers/3001-3500/3194-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 20*n^2 + 40*n - 60 for n>1.
> Conjectures from _Colin Barker_, Jul 02 2018: (Start)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 111 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

---

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 273 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A208429

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A208429>  
Result obtained: **7 September 2026**  
Paper: [5164](../papers/5001-5500/5164-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 3*a(n-1) +8*a(n-2) -18*a(n-3) -17*a(n-5) -116*a(n-6) +256*a(n-7) +184*a(n-8) -335*a(n-9) +260*a(n-10) -644*a(n-11) -368*a(n-12) +2013*a(n-13) -564*a(n-14) -1664*a(n-15) -1063*a(n-16) +1284*a(n-17) +3156*a(n-18) -1962*a(n-19) -256*a(n-20) +1020*a(n-21) -1448*a(n-22) -96*a(n-23) +144*a(n-24) +352*a(n-25) -128*a(n-26) for n>28

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Every cell the condition names lies a bounded number of lines away -- two of them, where it reaches a knight's move -- so a window of that width decides the condition on the line in the middle, and the lines with nothing far enough beyond them are decided at the end. Counting a line at a time therefore gives a transfer matrix with 38 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 28. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```
